Investigating the directly follows graph further, it might become evident that E directly following B– which occurred in 23 cases – is due to the specific property of the cases at hand. For instance, in an insurance process some employees after executingBmight find it important to consult their managers, who perform E, before the employees continue with C. Dependency graphs can highlight these rare cases as well, which can certainly be relevant during process analysis. While low threshold values in directly follows graphs provide all directly follows relationships and might lead to interesting insights, these graphs can become quite complex. In large-scale real-world applications, this can result in Spaghetti models, such as the one depicted in Figure 4.116. To reduce the size of the directly follows graph in the example, a threshold value ofd= 20 is discussed. This value leads to a less complex graph, which is shown inFigure 4.118(a). Still, some relations occur far less frequently than others, by a factor of 20 to 50. For instance,BfollowsA1103 times, while E follows B only 23 times. To abstract the graph even more,Figure 4.118(b)shows a directly follows graph with a high threshold value ofd= 900, which leads to dropping all edges with a frequency lower than 900. Notice that this value results in a graph that has exactly the same structure as the one that was created for a BPMN process diagram with an exclusive split, as shown inFigure 4.113. We can argue that this makes sense, since in most cases the process exhibits an exclusive split behaviour. In more than 2000 traces, the process has the exclusive split semantics, disregarding the few (fewer than 100) traces in which this is not the case.

####4.8.5 Interpreting Directly Follows Graphs
The structure of directly follows graphs is quite simple: There is an edge from A to B, ifBcan directly followA. The construction of those graphs from BPMN process models is straightforward. Hence, we could assume that both types of models represent the same process behaviour. However, this is not the case. There are two main reasons for this, which relate to paths and loops in directly follows graphs.

**Paths might not be Executions**
Graph-based process models are typically interpreted by taking a path from the start node to the end node. Returning to the BPMN process model shown in Figure 4.113(a), we start in nodeAand walk viaBandCtoF, terminating the process. The exclusive gateway asks us to follow just one path. In BPMN process models this works perfectly if the process is sequential, i.e., if there is no concurrency involved. Turning to the directly follows graph shown inFigure 4.113(b), we can do the same thing and follow the path from start node to end node, creating a complete process execution. In this case, a path is an execution. In general, if the underlying process does not exhibit any concurrency, a path from the start node to the end node in the directly follows graph represents a correct process execution. In case of concurrency, however, this is no longer true. Following a path from start to end might not yield a correct process execution. This observation is illustrated by an example. InFigure 4.114(b)it is not su”cient to walk a path from start node to end node, for instanceA, B, C, F. This is due to the fact thatDandE are not on that path, even though these activities need to be part of any process execution, as defined by the process model in Figure 4.114(a). At the same time, we can correctly interpret the BPMN process model inFigure 4.114(a), because the parallel split gateway defines concurrency between the branches. Hence, in BPMN process diagrams, a split is always associated with a particular type, such as exclusive or parallel. In directly follows graphs, this information is missing. There is no di!erence between exclusive split and parallel split, so paths might not be executions.

**Loops might not be Loops**
To investigate loops in directly follows graphs,Figure 4.119is considered. In part (a) of that figure, a BPMN process diagram with a loop involving activities B and C is shown. The set of traces that this process model allows can be characterized as follows. If the loop is not iterated, the execution results in traceABCD. A single iteration of the loop results in traceABCBCD,two iterations result in trace ABCBCBCD, etc. Using the directly follows relationships that are defined by that process model, the directly follows graph inFigure 4.119(b)is created. When we now take a path from the start node to the end node, only traces can be generated that also fit the process model, for instance the traces that we discussed above,ABCBCD and ABCBCBCD. This means that in this case a loop in the process model is correctly represented by a loop in the directly follows graph. But not only loops in the process model result in loops in the directly follows graph, but also concurrency. To illustrate this observation, the BPMN process diagram shown in Figure 4.114(a)is reconsidered. Since activities B and D are executed concurrently, they can directly follow each other. Consequently, the directly follows graph inFigure 4.114(b)contains a loop between Band D. We could interpret this loop as an iteration involving B and D. Walking the edges in the directly follows graph, we could create traces like ABDBDCF, ABCDEBCF, ABDBDCECF, ABCF. Some of these traces include loops, e.g.,ABDBDCF. However, the BPMN process model defines that each activity is executed exactly once! In particular, activity B is executed exactly once and activityDis executed exactly once. This property of traces based on the BPMN process model is violated by some of the traces that are generated by the directly follows graph, showing that the graph allows traces that are disallowed by the original process model. Since loops in directly follows graphs might not be loops in the process, it is important to exercise care when interpreting the execution semantics of directly follows graphs.

####4.8.6 Practical Considerations
This section covers practical considerations of directly follows graphs. These include preprocessing of event logs to filter out activities that are not required and a realistic example of a directly follows graph.

**Event Log Filtering**
Directly follows graphs play an important role in process mining, which is mainly due to their simple structure and the manifold ways to filter activities before deriving the graphs. The general structure is shown inFigure 4.120, where the steps involved and the data generated are represented by a BPMN process diagram. The first step involves the generation of a raw event log, using di!erent data sources of the target domain. Typically, data stored in the information systems of the organization is used, including relational databases, spread- sheets, and also unstructured text documents. The generation of event logs is a cumbersome effort during process mining projects; for more information about event log generation, the reader is referred to the bibliographical notes at the end of this chapter. Once the raw event log has been generated, filtering operations can be applied, which are discussed below. Once the filtered event log is available, a directly follows graph can be created, using the techniques introduced earlier in this section. Finally, the directly follows graph can be analyzed. We now discuss typical filtering operations that are performed as part of event log preprocessing. Notice that the technique to derive directly follows graphs is not affected by the filtering of event logs. In concrete application scenarios, it might be useful to filter out activities based on their frequency. In most cases, infrequent activities are filtered out, so that the resulting directly follows graph becomes less complex and concentrates only on the most frequent, i.e., most relevant activities. This filtering is guided by domain knowledge on which activities are relevant and which are not. To keep the presentation of directly follows graphs concise, this book represents the execution of an activity as an atomic step, without additional information. In real-world settings, however, the execution of an activity can be represented by a much richer structure. Attaching additional information to activity executions results in events which contain attributes. In general, there are two types of attributes, event attributes and case attributes. While event attributes describe particular properties of the event, case attributes describe properties of the case that the event is correlated to. Event attributes are particularly important in healthcare processes. An event that represents the execution of a blood sample analysis requires event attributes for the respective measurements, e.g., the hemoglobin value of the blood sample. Event attributes can be used during event log filtering, where only events can be retained that have a particular attribute, and all other events are filtered out. We might also use particular threshold values to concentrate only on events with event attributes in particular value ranges, for instance, a high hemoglobin value. Temporal information about events can be used for filtering as well, so that only events remain in the event log that have occurred during, for instance, night shifts. Case attributes are not associated with the event itself, but they describe the case as a whole. A typical example of a case attribute in a healthcare setting is the patient identifier. All events related to a particular patient have the same patient identifier. An event log can be filtered according to those case attributes, which effectively filters complete traces from the log, rather than individual events. In an online shopping scenario, we might be interested in the directly follows graph only for premium customers. In this case only traces are filtered and used that can be associated with premium customers, disregarding traces of normal customers. Notice that this section can only briefly sketch practical considerations related to event logs and process mining. The reader is referred to the bibliographical notes at the end of this chapter for references to the process mining literature.

**Sample Claim Handling Process**
To conclude this section, a more realistic example of a directly follows graph is discussed. The graph inFigure 4.121can be considered a variant of the claim handling process discussed earlier in this section. The directly follows graph is based on the execution of 3000 process instances, represented by 3000 traces in the event log. It has been created by the process mining tool Disco (https://fluxicon.com/disco/). Similarly to other process mining tools, the graphical representation of directly follows graphs is enhanced with a coloring of the nodes and weights of the edges, highlighting frequencies of the respective directly follows relations. In the figure, dark blue activities occur often, while grey activities occur comparatively seldom. According to the directly follows graph, the process is executed as follows. After receiving a claim, in all cases the claim is checked for completeness. With a threshold value 200, just two activities remain that directly follow the checking of claim completeness, namely sending an update request and reviewing the claim internally. Notice that the third outgoing edge to inviting a claim review does not meet the threshold and is being discarded for the moment. In most cases (2919), the claim is reviewed internally, possibly after sending update requests and receiving updates. Using the frequencies of the activities, it can be concluded that each claim is checked for completeness; in addition, a claim is also checked after an update has been received. This can be explained by the frequency of the check claim completeness activity (4331), being the sum of the frequencies of the receive claim (3000) and the receive update activities (1331). It is also nice to see that every send request is directly followed by the respective receiving of an update, i.e., there are no unanswered requests. In about half of the cases (1528), inviting a claim review directly follows the internal claim review, but taking a decision on the claim can also directly follow the internal review (observed in 1391 cases). In the lower part of the directly follows graph, the outcomes of the process are represented; slightly fewer than one third of the claims are accepted (891), while the other claims are rejected (2109). The less frequent directly follows relations are discussed next, i.e., the ones that occur fewer than 200 times. In rare cases (145), a claim review is invited directly following the completeness checking of the claim, i.e., without an internal review performed prior to reviewing the claim internally. These cases might provide interesting information during process analysis. To get hold of these findings, process analysts evaluate the concrete situations that have led to this particular directly follows relation, for instance, by interviewing the staff members involved. Another rare directly follows relation is from inviting a claim review to deciding on claim coverage, occurring just 20 times. One can assume that in those cases, claim reviews could not be received and the process would continue with taking the decision, even without those reviews. Again, process analysts might follow up on the specific reasons for this particular directly follows relation. Notice that other options include re-sending the review invitation or reviewing the claim again internally before proceeding. To conclude this section, a note on modelling theory is in order. Models can be differentiated according to their primary use indescriptiveor inprescriptive models. While descriptive models describe how a system or a process actually behaves, a prescriptive model prescribes how a system or a processshould behave. We can refer to directly follows graphs as descriptive models, since they describe the execution of concrete process instances. On the contrary, the process modelling languages introduced earlier in this section are typically used as prescriptive models, which prescribe how a process should be executed and not how a process was actually executed.

### 4.9 Declarative Process Modelling
The process modelling approaches introduced so far belong to the class of imperative languages. Loosely speaking, the execution semantics of a process model in an imperative language can be derived by following the edges of the graph. Typically, we begin in a start node and walk the edges of the graph to the final node. When the final node is reached, the process has terminated. This chapter has shown that di!erent process modelling languages use different terminologies. For instance, a start event in a BPMN process diagram closely resembles an initial place in a workflow net or a start task in a YAWL workflow specification. However, in all of those languages, a process execution is represented by walking the process model from start to end. These languages are called “imperative”, because the process model basically determines the next activity to take place. While imperative process modelling languages are widely used in academia and in industry and are therefore at the centre of attention in this textbook, there is another class of process modelling languages that is more suitable for defining less strict process behaviour. This is the class of declarative process modelling languages.

####4.9.1 Declarative Rules
In declarative process modelling, rules on the occurrence of events in process instances are defined. These rules collectively define a process model. Declarative process models are less restrictive than imperative ones, because anyprocess instance is allowed, as long as it does not violate any declarative rule. Rather than explicitly determining the next activities, declarative approaches allow anything, unless it is forbidden by some rule. This discussion shows the conceptual di!erence of declarative and imperative approaches to process modelling. There are several approaches to declarative process modelling, each of which defines a set of declarative rules. Hence, a wide variety of rules have been proposed. In this section we introduce the main ones, so that the key idea of declarative process modelling can be shown. These rules are explained as follows, whereAandBare process activities; a graphical representation of the declarative rules is shown inFigure 4.122. Response(A,B)defines that in each process instance,Boccurs afterA. In a sense,B is a “response” toA. Notice that this rule becomes active only if there is anAin the process instance. If there is noAin a given process instance, the rule is inactive, so thatBmay or may not be executed. This rule does not require one B for every A that has occurred, so that one B can be the response to several occurrences ofA. Precedence(A,B)defines thatB can only occur after A has occurred. Hence, a precedence between A and B is defined. As opposed to the response rule, in the precedence ruleBcannot occur withoutAhaving occurred before. At the same time,Bdoes not need to occur. Coexistence(A,B)is satisfied, ifAandBalways coexist in a process instance. This means that either neither of them occurs, or both occur. It is disallowed that one but not both ofAorBoccurs. The order ofAandB is irrelevant in this rule. Respondent-existence(A,B)defines that B occurs whenever A occurs. In this rule, B can occur before or afterAhas occurred. Still,Bcan occur without A having occurred. Not-coexistence(A,B)defines that A and B can never both occur in the same process instance. Not-succession(A,B)defines thatAis never followed byB. This includes directly follows relationships and alsoBfollowingAat a later point in time during the process instance. At the same time it is allowed thatB occurs before A. Existence(n 1 ,n 2 ,A)defines that in each process instance,Aoccurs at least n 1 ∈Nand at mostn 2 ∈ Ntimes, wheren 1 ≤n 2.

####4.9.2 Sample Declarative Process Model
The rules of a declarative process modelling language are now illustrated using the claim handling example that was discussed in the context of directly follows graphs in Section 4.8. While the BPMN process model shown inFigure 4.111(page 243) defines the process in a rather strict, imperative manner, declarative process modelling allows us to define the process in a more relaxed fashion, using declarative rules. All process executions are allowed that do not violate those rules.

R1:Claim Receivedoccurs exactly once (existence)
R2:Check Claimmust occur afterClaim Received(response)
The existence rule R1 defines that for each process instance, there is exactly one claim received event. Notice that instead of 1..1, we can use 1 as a short-hand notation. Rule R2 guarantees that there will be a check claim activity following the receiving of the claim. This appears to be very similar to an imperative process model. However, the declarative process model allows several executions of the check claim activity, which is not possible in the imperative process model shown inFigure 4.111. Notice that the rule only forces the process to check the claim at least once (after receiving the claim), while not limiting the number of those claim checks. This quite nicely shows the flavour of declarative process models. Rather than explicitly defining what is required, the rule just defines a minimal set of properties that have to be satisfied. But the process can do much more, as long as the rules are not violated. The next rule defines the relation between checking a claim and inviting external reviewers. It does not make any sense to invite external reviewers before checking the claim. At the same time, there can be cases in which no external reviews are required.
R3:Invite external Reviewcan only occur afterCheck Claim(precedence)
R4: An external review can only be received after it was invited, i.e.,Receive external Reviewcan only occur afterInvite external Review(precedence)
The precedence rule R3 allows inviting external reviewers only after checking the claim. Similarly to the discussion above, there can be several invitation activities, but none of them can appear before checking of the claim. Rule R4 defines that we can only receive a review, if we have invited a review before. Again, the declarative model allows us to receive several reviews for a given invitation. This might be the case, if a draft version of a review is sent before its final version. Notice that rule R4 allows missing reviews, which is not uncommon in real-world processes. The next rule covers the decision activity.
R5: A claim has to be checked before a decision can be taken, i.e.,Decide Claim Coveragecan only occur afterCheck Claim(precedence) Notice that R5 only defines that the decision can only occur after checking of the claim. It does not define that a decision has to be taken always. Depending on the modelling goal, this can be intended. For instance, during checking of the claim it may emerge that the information is incomplete, so that no decision can be taken. This rule allows several executions of the decision activity. Depending on the decision taken, either the claim is approved or rejected. Approving or rejecting the claim can only occur after taking the decision. It is evident that approving and rejecting the claim are mutually exclusive. These constraints can be expressed in the following rules.
R6: A claim can only be approved, if a decision activity was executed before, i.e.,Approve Claimcan only be executed afterDecide Claim Coverage (precedence)
R7: A claim can only be rejected, if a decision activity was executed before, i.e.,Reject Claimcan only be executed afterDecide Claim Coverage (precedence)
R8: A claim can be either approved or rejected, i.e.,Approve Claimand Reject Claimcan never be executed together in a given process instance (not-coexistence)
The resulting declarative process model is shown inFigure 4.123. To summarize, the declarative process model allows all execution sequences that are allowed by the BPMN process model shown inFigure 4.111. We now investigate whether it also allows the traces discussed in Section 4.8.4, which can happen in practice and which were disallowed by the imperative process model. As a side note, the end events in the BPMN diagram are not represented in the declarative model. In tracet 1 =〈CR, CC, IR, CC, DC, AC̸, the reviewer does not return the review, and the claim is checked again before a decision is taken. This trace is allowed by the declarative process model, because none of the rules R1 through R8 are violated. This also applies to tracet 2 =〈CR,CC,DC,CC,DC,RC̸, where there are two decision activities, but both of them occur after a check claim activity. Int 3 =〈CR, CC, IR, RR, DC, IR, RR, DC, AC̸, after the first decision activity, it turns out that an additional external review has to be invited. This behaviour of the process is perfectly in line with the declarative model, since any number of external reviews can be invited, as long as they appear after a check claim activity. The flexibility of the declarative model can also be seen by the missing edge between receiving a review and taking a decision. It is simply not required that the decision is based on an external review. It is possible that either no external review has been invited or it was invited but never received. The process model allows taking the decision, even though the external review is missing. In an imperative process model, the process would wait for the review, so that the process could get stuck. This discussion shows the higher degree of freedom and the increased responsibility for the people in charge of the process. To reflect those additional responsibilities, these persons are often referred to as knowledge workers. While providing a high degree of flexibility, declarative process models do provide guidance to knowledge workers by disallowing certain process be- haviours. To illustrate this observation, two additional traces are investigated, starting witht 4 =〈CR, CC, RR, DC, AC̸. In this trace, a claim is received and checked, before an external review is received. This trace is disallowed by the declarative process model, because an external review is received for which no review invitation was sent prior, violating rule R4. This limitation of the behaviour is intended, because receiving a review for which no invitation was sent might expose a case of fraud. To conclude this section,t 5 =〈CR, CC, IR, RR, DC, DC, AC, RC̸is investigated. It represents a case in which a claim is received and checked, before a review is invited and received. The decision activity is executed twice, before the claim is accepted and rejected. Obviously, this trace violates the declarative process model. It does so, not because there are two decision activities, but due to both accepting and rejecting the claim, violating R8.

## 5 Data and Decisions
The previous chapter discussed key concepts of business processes and the most prominent modelling languages to express them. The primary focus was on activities and their causal ordering. In fact, these are the only aspects covered by Petri-net-based process modelling languages as introduced in Section 4.2. While traditional business process modelling provides an important cornerstone to facilitate analysis, improvement, and enactment of business processes, it fails to properly support two important aspects that nearly every business process has: data and decisions. In this chapter, data and decisions in business processes are investigated. In particular, we show how UML class diagrams can specify the structure of data objects that are involved in business processes. Data is a key ingredient of decisions, which is discussed in the context of the Decision Model and Notation standard, which complements the BPMN standard.

### 5.1 Data in Business Processes
To motivate the role of data in business processes, Section 5.1.1 introduces an example of an order handling process that shows various aspects related to data in business processes. Section 5.1.2 provides a brief introduction to UML class diagrams, and it shows how data objects in business processes and class diagrams are related. In Section 5.1.3, the behaviour of data objects related to the states they can assume and to the state transitions they can perform is investigated. This section concludes by discussing the execution semantics of input sets and output sets of process activities.

####5.1.1 Motivating Example
The motivating example is an extension of the order handling process discussed in Section 4.7. The process model shown inFigure 5.1is more complex, since it involves data objects and data object collections. To illustrate the role of data in business processes, the order handling process is discussed. A start message event occurs when an order is received. This message contains a data objectOrder. Notice that the label of this data object is o:Order, which indicates that data objectois an instance of the classOrder, which is discussed below in the context of class diagrams associated with process models. Once the order is received, it is accepted, and a collection of order items i is created, i.e., the items that have been ordered. Each item is checked for availability, which is done by theCheck Availability activity. This activity creates as output two collections. As will be discussed below in the context of data object states, these collections reflect the itemsi1 that are available and the items i2 that are not available. On the upper branch of the inclusive split, the available items are packed in a parcel p1 before they are sent. Notice that for sending the parcel, an input data objectc:Customer is required, marked by an arrow in the upper left corner of that data object. As was discussed in Section 4.7, input data refers to data that is available before the process starts. We assume that customer information is available before the order is received. The lower branch is responsible for backordering items in the list i2 of missing items, before those items can be packed in a parcel and sent. Finally the order is archived and the process terminates. This process assumes that at least one item is ordered, and that all missing items can be backordered. The behaviour of the process is specified by an inclusive split, since there are three options: Some items are available, while other items are not available, which results in both branches of the inclusive split being executed. This means that neither the collection i1 nor the collection i2 is empty. All items that have been ordered are available. In this case only the upper branch is executed. This means thati2 is empty, while there are some items in i1. None of the items ordered is available, so that all items have to be back-ordered before being packed and sent. As a result,i1 is empty, while i2 is not. Notice that all these di!erent cases are very well covered by the inclusive split. This could be represented by an expressioni 13 =∅on the upper outgoing edge of the inclusive split, and by an expressioni 23 =∅on its lower outgoing edge. Because we assume that each order contains at least one item, at least one branch will be taken and the process will not get stuck.

####5.1.2 Data Objects and Class Diagrams
This section explores the relationship between data objects in process models on the one hand and data models on the other hand; UML class diagrams are used to represent those data models. Exploring this relationship is important, since data objects involved in business processes are typically stored in information systems, which rely on data models. A focus on data models is important for process automation as well. If a process engine should access data objects stored in the database of an organization, the data model can serve as a link to the concrete database schema. Investigating the order handling process inFigure 5.1from a data perspective, we can identify the following data objects.

- o: Order: The data objectorepresents an order.
- c: Customer: The customer who has submitted the order.
- i, i1, i2: Lists of Items: There are up to three data object collections that represent lists of items, whereirepresents the list of all items andi1 and i2 the lists of items that are available and not available, respectively.
- p1, p2: Parcel: There are up to two parcels required to deliver the ordered items, where items in the listi1 are sent in parcelp1, while items ini2 are sent in parcel p2.
```
The data model involving customers, orders, items, and parcels is shown in Figure 5.2. In UML class diagrams, the structure of objects is represented by classes, which are shown as rectangles, marked with the name of the class. Properties of objects are represented by attributes, which are shown in the lower compartment. Notice that a simplified version of UML class diagrams is used, which does not include behavioural aspects, such as the methods that an object can execute. For more information on UML class diagrams, the reader is referred to the bibliographical notes at the end of this chapter. The attributes of the customer class represent the customer identifier and her name and address information. Each order stores an order identifier, the identifier of the customer who placed that order, and the date on which the order was placed. Each item holds information to identify the item, the respective order, and the parcel the item is sent with. Finally, the parcel stores a parcel identifier, the identifier of the order the parcel is associated with, and the weight of the parcel. In class diagrams, relationships between objects are modeled by associations between classes. For instance, each customer can have an arbitrary number of orders. It is allowed that a customer does not have any orders. These properties of relationships are represented by so-called multiplicities. For instance, 1 on the left-hand side of the association between customer and order represents the fact that each order belongs to exactly one customer, while 0..* denotes that a customer can have any number of orders, including no orders. Along these lines, an order can have one or more items, while each item belongs to exactly one order. For ease of presentation, the concrete goods are represented by the item class, rather than articles, i.e., items represent the instance level rather than the type level. Finally, each parcel has at least one item, and each item is delivered in exactly one parcel. At the same time, each parcel belongs to exactly one order, while each order has at least one parcel. After class diagrams have been introduced, a concrete database involving customers, orders, items, and parcels is presented. This database represents the state of the system after several process instances have been executed. This database also contains the attributes that we need to show associations between classes, for instance the customer identifier attribute cid in the order class. The database is shown in Figure 5.3. As we can see in the customer table, there are three customers, two of whom reside in Leipzig, while one customer lives in Houston. There are five orders. Using the customer identifiercidin the order table, it becomes evident that the first two orders (withoid o273ando343) have been placed by Easton, the customer withcid=c124. The third and fifth orders (o411ando422) have been placed by Thomson, while the fourth order (o421) belongs to Jones. Investigating the ordered items, the ordero273 contains a hair dryer, a pullover, and a lipstick, while ordero343 consists of a mixer and a coffee maker. In order o411, a pair of jeans and an umbrella have been ordered. The order o421 by Jones contains a Swiss knife, a snowboard, and a screwdriver, while o422 consists of a mug. The item table also contains information about the association between items and parcels. In particular, the hairdryer is shipped in parcel p122, while the pullover and the lipstick are shipped in parcel p121. All items in the order o343 are shipped in parcel p131. Each of the five orders in the database corresponds to one process instance. To illustrate the role of data in one particular process instance, the ordero421 is investigated in detail. The process instance that corresponds to this order is shown inFigure 5.4. To illustrate data values in process instances, the BPMN notation is used. While this not the intended use of BPMN, we can illustrate the role of data objects by associating particular data values stored in the database with data objects in the process instance. For instance, rather than using a generic data objecto:Order, a particular data object instance is used, such aso421:Order. Notice that this approach resembles object diagrams as defined in the UML, where a particular objectc1 of a class C can be represented by c1: C, including a graphical notation. We encounter the same situation, since a specific order with oido421 was received, which is indicated by the label o421:Order in Figure 5.4. Along these lines, the activity nodes in Figure 5.4 represent activity instances, rather than activity models. Hence, these nodes are marked just like objects in object diagrams, with a leading colon. Therefore, e.g., the activity instance that reads ordero421is marked with:Accept Order, indicating one particular activity instance. The start event writes the order object o421, which is read by the:Accept Orderactivity instance, to extract the ordered items. The three items ordered are shown in the list data object. Checking the availability of the ordered items, two lists are created. The available items list contains i8821 and i5232; these items can be packed in parcel p142 and sent. Item i1212, however, needs to be backordered, before it is packed in parcel p143 and sent to the customer, using the address stored in the customer data object c123. This discussion illustrates the role of data during process executions. It also sketches a particular aspect related to lists of data objects and their relationships to multi-instance process activities. In BPMN process diagrams, we can define data objects as collections, meaning that several objects of the same type are being processed. This feature was used inFigure 5.1 to represent the items that an order is composed of. Data object collections are also present in particular process instances, as shown inFigure 5.4. The input to the check availability activity is a collection composed of three items. During the checking of the item availability, the data collection is partitioned into a list of available items and a list of items that are not available. As shown inFigure 5.4, itemi1212 is not available and needs to be backordered, while the other two items are available and can be packed and shipped immediately. If a data object collection is input to a multiple instances activity, then the number of instances of that activity is identical to the cardinality of the input collection. In the example, the list consists of just a single item, so that the Backorder Itemactivity has just one activity instance. However, if three items needed to be backordered, there would be three instances of theBackorder Itemactivity. With multiple instances activities, the cardinality of the output data object collection is identical to the number of activity instances, which is identical to the cardinality of the input data collection.

####5.1.3 Data Object Lifecycles
The example introduced in the previous section shows that data objects in business processes can be related to classes in UML class diagrams. In this section we show that not only the structure of objects and their relationships to other objects can be specified, but also their behaviour. The behaviour of objects is represented by states that objects can assume and state transitions that objects can perform. In the context of this textbook, we assume that state transitions of data objects are done by executing process activities. Figure 5.5 shows a version of the order handling process which features states that objects can adopt during process execution. After an order is received, an order data object is written. As can be seen by the state information, the order is in thevalidstate. Hence, this process assumes that valid orders are received. After accepting the order, the state of the order is changed to accepted, and a list of items is written, each of which is in theorderedstate. The next activity checks for each item whether it is in stock or not, writing the respective lists of items that are available (listi1) and that are not available (listi2). All items that are in the stateavailablecan be packed in a parcel, which writes a new parcel data objectp1 in stateprepared. Using the parcel and the customer information in stateconfirmedas input, the parcel can be sent. Items that are not available are backordered, resulting in a state change toavailable. Now that those missing items have been backordered and are available, they can be packed, and the parcel is sent. Finally, the process is completed, and the order reaches thearchivedstate. This discussion shows the states that data objects can be in, and it also shows state transitions. Those states and state transitions can be represented by state transition diagrams, called object lifecycles. The object lifecycles of the data objects involved in the order handling process are shown inFigure 5.6. Each object lifecycle is defined by an individual state transition diagram, which consists of a set of states, one particular start state, and transitions. The start state is marked by an incoming arc, indicating the state in which the object is created. Transition labels refer to process activities. For instance, the Accept orderactivity reads an order object in the state valid and writes the order object in the stateaccepted, thereby performing the respective state transition of the order data object. Carefully investigating the state transitions, we find process activity labels which do not occur in the process model shown in Figure 5.5. This is due to the fact that, in general, data objects are accessed by many different business processes. For instance, in another business process, a received order can be checked, which results in an order data object either in thevalid or in the invalid state. In general, whenever there are two or more transitions with the same label A that originate from a given state, the process activityAhas different options to operate. A can change the state of the data object to any follow-up state that is labelled with that activity. In the example, theCheck Orderactivity has two options: either change the state of the order data object to invalid or change it tovalid. Furthermore, invalid orders can enter either thevalidor the rejected state. Notice that these state changes cannot be performed by the process at hand, but certainly by other processes of the organization.

####5.1.4 Input/Output Sets
To conclude this section, the execution semantics of data in business processes is addressed. We do so by discussing a variant of the order handling process introduced earlier in this section; it is shown in Figure 5.7. Notice that the example is based on the same data objects as the previous example, including classes (as shown inFigure 5.2) and object lifecycles (Figure 5.6). This variant of the process receives an order that is not checked yet. The Check Orderactivity can change the state either toinvalidor tovalid. In general, the output sets of an activity determine which data objects that activity can write in which states. More concretely, an output set of an activity consists of a set of data objects, each of which is in a particular state. Each activity can have many output sets; during its execution, the activity determines which output set is used. In the example, theCheck Order activity has two output sets, each of which consists of a data object and a state,{Order[invalid]},{Order[valid]}. During execution, theCheck Orderactivity decides which output set to use. In this case, either the first output set or the second output set is used, indicating an invalid and a valid order, respectively. State information can be used to decide which branch of an exclusive split to take. If the order is in theinvalidstate, the upper branch is selected, and the order is rejected. If, however, the order is in thevalidstate, it is accepted, following the lower branch. An activity can not only generate different outcomes, it can also read different inputs, which is represented by input sets. An input set consists of a set of data objects in specific states. An activity can have many input sets. To make this discussion more concrete, the example shown inFigure 5.7 is discussed. TheSend Responseactivity has two input data objects, relating to the order either in staterejectedor in stateaccepted. (To avoid complex graph structures in the process diagram, theOrder[accepted]data object is drawn a second time.) The input sets of theSend Response activity are {Order[rejected]},{Order[accepted]}. Input sets have implications on the enablement of activities. We assume that the control flow indicates that an activity is already enabled, i.e., it is control-flow enabled. Control-flow enablement is a necessary condition for an activity to be enabled, but it is not su”cient. In addition, an activity needs to be data-flow enabled. Data-flow enablement is based on input sets. In particular, an activity is data-flow enabled, if the data objects of (at least) one of its input sets are available in the respective states. Returning to the Send Responseactivity, it is control-flow enabled either after the order has been rejected or after the order has been accepted. Assuming the order is rejected, one input set of that activity is available, i.e., the order in state rejected. Hence, Send Responseis data-flow enabled and can be executed with the input set{Order[rejected]}. Notice that input sets and output sets of activities need to be in line with the object lifecycles of the data objects involved. Following the order data object inFigure 5.7, initially each order is in thereceivedstate. If there is a state transition to invalid, then there will be a state transition to rejected. In general, whenever there is a data objectd[s] in the input set of an activity A ,d[s′] can be in the output set of that activity only if there is a state transition in the object lifecycle of d labelled A which brings the data object from statesto states′. This observation is illustrated inFigure 5.7, where, for instance, theAccept Order activity hasOrder[valid] in its input set andOrder[accepted] in its output set. This is correct, because there is a state transition labelledAccept Orderfromvalidtoacceptedin the object lifecycle of orders, as depicted in Figure 5.6. This example shows a very simple scenario for input sets and output sets, where just a single input object and just a single output object have been used. In general, activities can use multiple data objects in input sets and in output sets, making those sets an important asset in the definition of data aspects in business processes.

### 5.2 Decisions in Business Processes
It is an interesting observation that business process management in general and business process modelling in particular traditionally have neglected decisions. Since basically any business process contains decisions, it should not come as a surprise that decision modelling and execution came into the focus of business process management recently. In this chapter, we extend the scope of business processes with decisions. In particular, Decision Model and Notation (DMN) is discussed, which complements BPMN with decision modelling. To illustrate the role of decisions in business processes, the BPMN process diagram shown inFigure 5.8is investigated. This figure shows a business process from finance, which starts when a credit card application is received. After recording the application information, the process has to decide on the basic credit card eligibility of the applicant. This decision is represented in BPMN by a business rule taskDetermine Basic Credit Card Eligibility; in the remainder of this chapter, we will refer to business rule tasks also as decision tasks. The decision taken by that task is used to guide the process flow. If the applicant is not eligible for the credit card, her application is rejected. Otherwise, a second decision is taken to ultimately decide on the application. A second exclusive or gateway is used to either send a rejection letter or to send an acceptance letter of the credit card application. This example shows that decisions are—to some extent—represented in BPMN already, as decision tasks and as di!erent outgoing edges of an exclusive or gateway. The decision logic, however, cannot be defined in BPMN. Still, business processes have to take decisions, as illustrated by the credit card example. To discuss the role of decision taking during process executions, the architecture of process oriented information systems shown in Figure 3.37on page 124 has to be revisited. During process execution, the process engine keeps track of the execution semantics of the business process. For each activity, the engine invokes the respective service, as defined in the implemented process model. In general, there are two general approaches for decision making in business processes, automated decision making by a software service and human decision making. In the business process shown in Figure 5.8, we focus on the first decision, i.e., the decision task Determine Basic Credit Card Eligibility. When this task is executed, the process engine invokes a service provider. Depending on the configuration of the system, this can be either a software service or a human employee who takes the decision based on the data available and her personal experience. The decision taking by a software service is illustrated in Figure 5.9(a). When the decision task needs to be executed, the process engine invokes the software service with the parameters provided by the credit card application. After completion, the software service provides a return value, in this case either yes or no. This output is used by the process engine to decide on the continuation of the process: Either thenobranch of theexclusive or gateway is followed and a rejection message is sent or the yes branch is followed and the full eligibility check is conducted to ultimately decide on the credit card application. Due to increasing auditing requirements that companies face today, it is important to document any decision making activity properly. To this end, for any decision that is taken during the execution of a business process, the following questions have to be answered. 

1. How exactly is a decision taken?
2. What data is used to take the decision?
3. What policies are taken into account?
4. Who is responsible for taking a decision?
In the credit card application example, these questions are not completely answered. The first question looks at how exactly the decision was taken. In the first implementation scenario, the decision was determined by the software code that implements the software service. While we can say that it is clear how the decision was taken on a software code level, software code is typically too low-level to communicate decision making to a potential auditor in a comprehensible way. Similar considerations apply to the second question, which data was used? This question can be answered on a technical level that mentions database tables and the respective queries to access them, but this might also not be suited for an auditor. Additional policies can be regarded as input to a software engineering project, which subsumes a variety of software requirements and software design documents. Ultimately it will be very hard to trace back the software code to the particular policies that govern decision making. The last question relates to the person responsible for the decision taken. If the decision is taken by a software service, no person is even involved during process execution. However, a set of persons might be candidates for being responsible, including requirements engineers of the software development project and software developers that actually coded the service. In any case, the information to answer these questions is disparate and can only be traced back with significant e!ort, if this is at all possible. In many cases, decisions are taken by humans, which is illustrated inFigure 5.9(b)by buttonsyes andno. Returning to the questions of decision making, in human decision taking, we can easily identify the person responsible for the decision. However, we do not know in detail, how the decision was taken or which policy was used by that person. Since knowledge workers can use additional information that might not even be stored in the information systems of the organization, we cannot confidently answer the question about the data being used during decision taking. These shortcomings in documenting decision taking during process execution have contributed to the development and to the success of the recent standard Decision Model and Notation that will be discussed in the next section. Based on the design principles and the language elements, we will return to the questions on decision taking and the role of DMN in answering them.

#### 5.2.1 Decision Model and Notation Overview
DMN is a sibling standard to the well-known and widely used BPMN standard. Its goal is to provide the expressive means to model decisions, both the structure of decisions and the concrete decision logic. In Section 5.2.2, we introduce the DMN by investigating decision requirements diagrams, which we can use to provide a structuring of decisions and related artefacts, such as data input and organizational policies. Decision logic is in the centre of Section 5.2.3, where the main focus is on decision tables, the most widely used technique to represent decision logic. Before the modelling elements of the DMN are introduced, a characterization of the types of decisions that can be expressed in DMN is appropriate. In the daily business of an organization, many decisions are taken. These decisions are of different nature, ranging from operational decisions to strategic decisions. An example of an operational decision is whether or not a credit card can be granted; this example was introduced earlier in this chapter. Operational decisions are characterized by a quite narrow scope, so that the data required and the decision logic is known before the decision is taken. An example of a strategic decision is to which market an insurance company should expand over the next five years. This decision is of strategic nature, and it is much broader and more complex than the operational decision discussed above. We will probably not be able to provide a concrete logic formula for the strategic decision; there will always be factors that are unknown. In the business world, these decisions are typically taken by the management, typically based on the experience of the acting management. To provide accountability of decision making and a basis for decision automation, DMN is designed to represent operational decisions, i.e., those decisions for which we know the required data input and how the decision output depends on the input, i.e., the decision logic.

#### 5.2.2 Decision Requirements Diagram
DMN provides decision requirements diagrams that allow us to model the structure of decisions and the artefacts involved. These diagrams are graphs that consist of nodes and edges of the types shown inFigure 5.10. Decision: Decisions are the core elements of decision requirements diagrams; decisions use input data and decision logic to compute output values. Input data: Input data represents data objects that are used by the decision as input. Business knowledge: A business knowledge node provides decision logic that can be reused by di!erent decisions in decision requirements diagrams, using an invocation  mechanism. This concept is useful if decision logic is shared by several decisions. Knowledge source: Knowledge sources are important to document the rea- sons for a specific decision logic. These will not directly be used during decision taking, but knowledge sources are important to provide transparency in decision management, which is an essential aspect of auditing requirements. Nodes of these types can be connected in DMN decision requirements diagrams by edges of di!erent types: Information requirements: Information requirements connect input data nodes to decisions, defining that the input data is used as an input to the decision. Edges of this type can also relate a sub-decision to a decision, meaning that the output of the sub-decision serves as an input to the decision. Knowledge requirements: Knowledge requirements connect business knowledges to decisions, meaning that the business knowledge is used to take the decision. Authority requirements: Authority requirements connect knowledge sources to decisions and to business knowledge nodes, indicating any policy or legal regulation that guides the decision making. These modelling elements are illustrated by an example shown inFigure 5.11. This diagram provides a high-level view of the decision that lies behind the decision taskDetermine Basic Credit  The diagram shows that the employment status of the applicant and her debt history are input parameters to determine the basic credit card eligibility. This decision also requires the output of theVerify applicant identity decision, which—due to its relative position—serves as a sub-decision. This sub-decision requires identification documents, as shown by its input data and the information requirements edge. The example also allows us to discuss business knowledge and knowledge sources.Verify identityis an example of a business knowledge node, which provides the decision logic to evaluate theVerify applicant identitydecision. When this decision has to be taken, its input data are used as input parameters to the business knowledge, which evaluates the decision and returns an output value to theVerify applicant identitydecision. This value travels along the information requirements edge to the top-level decision. It provides the third input value that the top-level decision requires. Knowledge sources provide documentation information about the decision logic, not the decision logic itself. In the example, theBasic CCE policyguides the top-level decision, while theIdentity verification policydefines the rules that are used by the business knowledgeVerify identity. While knowledge sources do not define decision logic directly, they provide important means to answer some of the questions that were introduced earlier in this section. Decision requirements diagrams allow us to answer some of the questions raised above. By showing the relations between decisions and sub-decisions and input data used by these, we can answer question (2), related to the data to be used during decision taking. By investigating the knowledge sources in a decision requirements diagram, we can also answer question (3), which is related to the reasoning behind a certain decision and the respective policies. Question (4) about the responsibility of decision taking can also be answered by decision requirements diagrams. Just like BPMN, DMN allows us to define various attributes for modelling elements. In particular, each decision in a decision requirements diagram features attributes to characterize roles that are involved in decision making.
decisionMaker: The decision maker attribute allows us to define one or more roles or organizational units that are responsible for making the decision. An example of a decision maker in the credit card example is a member of the credit card application department. decisionOwner: The decision owner attribute defines the organizational role that is responsible for decision taking. In the example, the head of the credit card application department is the owner of theDetermine Basic Credit Card Eligibility decision.
To summarize, decision requirements diagrams allow us to answer questions (2), (3), and (4). To address question (1) on how decisions are actually taken, different ways to define decision logic in DMN will be covered in the next section.

####5.2.3 Decision Logic
Decision logic describes how the output of a decision is derived from its input. In general, so-called value expressions are used to define decision logic. The DMN standard distinguishes between di!erent types of value expressions. Literal expression: One form of literal expression is defining decision logic by text. Literal expressions can also be used to define decision logic formally precise, for instance, through program code. Decision Table: Decision tables are widely used to define decision logic. Decision tables will be covered in Section 5.3. Invocation: Invocations are a mechanism to use existing decision logic, provided by a business knowledge node. Despite the fact that DMN mainly targets operational decisions that can be executed automatically, the language also allows us to define decision logic in an informal, imprecise manner, using English text as literal expression. In an apartment rental scenario, the landlord might define her decision logic as follows. I will accept an applicant if he or she has a good employment history and a good credit rating. The applicant must not have any pets. All supporting documents must be authentic. And, on top, the applicant must be a nice person. Since we cannot operationally formalize the precise meaning of having agood employment historyor of being anice person, this decision can only be taken by the landlord using her personal opinion and taste. This decision cannot be taken automatically. Despite the formal imprecision of the decision logic, DMN can still be used to define the decision, using English text. We can also define in a decision requirements diagram the input data that is required for taking the decision, including the required documents by the applicant. As a consequence, both formally precise and imprecise decisions are covered by DMN. Literal expressions can also be defined in a formal manner. DMN provides a dedicated language, called Friendly Enough Expression Language, or FEEL. An example using FEEL expressions is shown inFigure 5.12, which contains a decision requirements diagram to determine the net income from the gross income and the legal status of a tax payer. For convenience, the expressions are associated with the respective decision in the diagram; formally, decision logic is not part of decision requirements diagrams. The decision requirements diagram shows a decisionDetermine Net In- come, with a sub-decisionSocial Contributionsthat calculates an output value based on its input valuesLegal StatusandGross Income. The decision logic is defined by an expression, using anif-clause. If the legal status of the tax payer is married, then the social contributions amount to 35% of her gross income, otherwise to 42% of her gross income. Notice that the value of the expression is not explicitly assigned to some variables; it is just passed as the output to the decision that determines the net income, which is done by subtracting the social contributions from the gross income. This example also shows that a decision can also be considered as a computation. Again, the computation is well documented by the decision requirements diagram.

### 5.3 Decision Tables
Due to their ability to provide decision logic in a compact and formal, yet readable form, decision tables are widely used to define decision logic in decision models. Figure 5.13 shows a process collaboration that involves a client and an insurance company. The collaboration starts when the client sends an insurance claim. The insurance company receives the claim and registers it, before the decision is taken whether or not to cover the claim. This decision is represented by theDecide on Claim Coveragedecision task. Depending on the decision taken, either a letter of approval or a letter of rejection is prepared and sent. Before the decision logic can be discussed in detail, the process model needs to be enhanced with data objects. While this is not strictly required, it allows us to provide an association between data objects in the process model and input data used during decision taking. The business process of the insurance company including data objects is shown in Figure 5.14. It contains two data objects, representing the claim and the customer contract, respectively. The decision task reads both data objects, as defined by the arcs between the respective data objects and the decision task. From a process modelling perspective, data objects do not have any internal structure. In the example, we do not know the structure of the claim, neither do we know the attributes in the customer contract. Since decision logic uses specific attributes of data objects, in general, data objects in processes cannot be directly used as input to decisions. In the example, we only know that information about the claim and information about the customer contract are required to take the decision on claim coverage. We do not know which attributes contain the required information. During the definition of the decision logic, this information has to be retrieved from the information systems that store and maintain the data objects. To illustrate these concepts in a concrete scenario, we assume that the process covers bike thefts. In this setting, the claim data object contains an attributeValuethat refers to the value of the stolen bike; an attributeTheft- Timecontains the time of day when the bike was stolen. TheCustomer Contractdata object contains attributes for name and address of the customer, and also the type of contract. The latter is represented by an attributeType. The information stored in the attributes of the data objects can be used in decision requirements diagrams. Figure 5.15 shows the resulting diagram, defining that theDecide on Claim Coveragedecision hasClaim.Value, Claim.TheftTime, andCustomerContract.Typeas input data. After discussing the structure of input data objects, the structure of decision tables is addressed. The columns in a decision table refer to data input and also the output of the decision. While the DMN standard allows multiple output columns, we will concentrate on decision tables with a single output. The rightmost column of a decision table refers to the output of the decision. The decision table of theDecide on Claim Coveragedecision is shown in Figure 5.16. We can observe that the data input shown in the decision requirements diagram can be found in the decision table as well. Decision taking depends on the value of the claim, on the theft time of the bike, and on the customer contract type. The rows of the table define the decision logic. DMN allows us to define different hit policies that tell us exactly how to interpret the rows in a decision table. In the example, the letterF represents theFirsthit policy. This hit policy defines that the rows are evaluated in order, and the first row that matches the input values will be selected, and the output value will be returned. Other hit policies provided by the DMN will be covered in the next section. In general, each row in a decision can be considered as a conjunction of conditions. Each input column refers to a condition, and all conditions have to be met for a row to be selected. The fields of input columns can contain values, intervals and other types of expressions. The hyphen symbol in a field of an input column means that any value for the respective input data is allowed. To illustrate the evaluation of decision tables, a case of a silver customer with claim value 850 e and theft time 18 (6 o’clock in the afternoon) is considered. Starting in row 1, we evaluate if the claim value is between 0 and 200; since this is not true, the first row cannot be selected, and we can move to the second row. Again, the claim value does not match the interval for that row. We have to move on until row 5 to satisfy the first condition relating to the claim value. Since the bike was stolen at 6 o’clock in the evening, also the theft time condition is satisfied by row 5. However, the customer type condition is violated, because this option is available only for gold customers. Moving on to row 6, all conditions are met for our case, so that the decision returns yes and the claim can be covered. The process model shown in Figure 5.14takes the return value yes of the decision task and uses it to decide on the outcome of the data-based exclusive or split. Finally, the letter of approval is prepared, and the claim is approved. This example shows the interaction between business processes and decisions. The decision table specifies the decision logic in a concise and comprehensible way. All thefts until a claim value of 200eare covered. Claims with theft value between 200eand 500eare covered for gold customers. Claims of this value range for silver customers are only covered if they occurred between 6 o’clock in the morning and 10 o’clock in the evening. Claims in that value range are covered for bronze customers in a narrower theft time window. Claims up to 1000eare only covered for silver and gold customers, where the theft time window of silver customers is again narrower than that of gold customers. No claims exceeding 1000 e are covered.

### 5.4 Hit Policies
The motivating example discussed in the previous section illustrates how decision logic can be expressed in decision tables. The rows of the decision table were evaluated in order, the first row that matched was selected, and the corresponding output value was returned. Therefore, this policy of evaluating decision tables is called the First hit policy. DMN provides a rich set of hit policies, which are discussed next. The hit policies can be distinguished into single hit policies and multi-hit policies. In single hit policies, for each possible combination of input values, there is at most a single row that is selected. Even if a given input combination matches multiple rows, a single hit policy makes sure that only one row is selected. We assume that for each input combination, at least one row matches. This is a valid assumption, because otherwise input combinations would exist for which the decision could not provide an output value. Decision tables with this property are called complete.

####5.4.1 Single Hit Policies
To provide a precise understanding of the hit policies, we assume a decision table with n input columnsI 1 ,I 2 ,...,Inand one output columnO. Each input combination i is an element of the Cartesian product of the input columns, i.e.,i∈I 1 ×I 2 ×...×In. In other words,iis an n-tuple that consists of one value for each input column. The decision table hasmrowsR 1 ,R 2 ,...Rm. Let matches(Ri) be the set of input combinations that match the row Ri, and letout(Ri) be the output value of Ri. These definitions are applied to the decision table shown in Figure 5.16. Since there are three input columns,n= 3, such thatI 1 =Claim.V alue, I 2 = Claim.T hef tT ime, andI 3 =CustomerContract.T ype. Since there are seven rows in the decision table,m= 7. A claim by a silver customer with claim value 850eand theft time 18 is one possible input combination. Formally, the claim can be represented by a tuple i = (850e, 18 , silver) ∈Claim.V alue×Claim.T hef tT ime× CustomerContract.T ype. We now investigate which rows this tuple matches. Since the claim value exceeds 200, the tuple does not match the first row. As a consequence,i3∈ matches(R 1 ). The same holds for rows 2 through 5. The first row that matches is row 6, i.e.,i∈matches(R 6 ). As a result, the output valueout(R 6 )=yes of row 6 is returned for the input combination i. Based on these notions, the single hit policies are characterized as follows. Let i, j∈I 1 ×I 2 ×...×Inbe input combinations, and letR 1 ,R 2 ,...Rm be the rows of the decision table. Unique (U): In theUniquehit policy, for each input combination, exactly one row matches, i.e.,i∈matches(Rk)⇔i3∈matches(Rl), 1 ≤k 3 =l≤ m. Any (A): TheAny hit policy allows several matches for a given input combination; however, these matches need to agree on the output value, so that ‘any’ match can be selected to derive the output value: i∈matches(Rk)∩matches(Rl)=⇒ out(Rk)=out(Rl), 1 ≤k, l≤m. Priority (P): ThePriority hit policy allows several rows to match for a given input combination, and these do not need to agree on the output value. The output value to be returned is determined by the ordering of output values in the output domain. First (F): TheFirsthit policy shares the assumptions with the previous hit policy. However, the output is based on the ordering of the rows, so that the first row that matches determines the output of the decision. Based on these characterizations of hit policies, we can check for a given decision table if the respective hit policy is satisfied. Since theFirst and Priority hit policies do not restrict matching in decision tables, all decision tables can be combined with these hit policies. However, the other hit policies might be violated by a decision table. The Unique hit policy imposes the strongest requirements on a decision table, since each input combination matches exactly one row. A decision table with this property also satisfies theAnyhit policy. The reverse is not true, because the Any hit policy allows multiple rows to match for a given input combination, which Unique for bids. The decision table in Figure 5.16 satisfies the First hit policy. However, it does not satisfy the Unique hit policy, because there exist input combinations which match multiple rows. Due to the last row in the table, actually, many input combinations have multiple matches. This applies, for instance, to the case of the silver customer with claim value 850e and theft time 18 which matches rows 6 and 7. The Any hit policy allows an input combination to match several rows. However, this hit policy is also violated, since rows 6 and 7 have different output values (yes, no), so that we cannot use any of them to compute the output of the decision. To solve this problem, row 7 can be changed to claim values in excess of 1000e. The resulting table is shown inFigure 5.17. With this modification, the table satisfies theUniquehit policy and, thereby, also theAnyhit policy. Unfortunately, the table is no longer complete, since there are input combinations which do not match any row. An example is the case of a silver customer whose 850e-bike was stolen at 8 o’clock in the evening, at theft time 20. This input combination neither matches row 6 (because theft time 20 is not covered) nor the modified row 7 (claim value does not exceed 1000e). In order to provide a decision table that satisfies theUniquehit policy and that is complete, several rows have to be added. These rows reflect the decision outcomenofor the di!erent value intervals. To cover all cases of silver customers with claim value between 501 e and 1000 ethat are outside of the covering theft time interval, a dedicated row ([501e;1000e], ]18;8[, silver, no) has to be added. Notice that expressions of the form ]l;u[ denote the open interval of all valuesvsuch thatl<v<u. When considering time of day, ]18; 8[ defines all thefts that occur after 6 o’clock in the evening and before 8 o’clock in the morning. Notice that row 10 of that decision table also covers the case of a claim with value 850e, theft time 20 for a silver customer, which was not covered in the previous version of the decision table shown in Figure 5.17. The resulting decision table is shown in Figure 5.18.

####5.4.2 Multi-Hit Policies
The DMN standard also allows us to define multi-hit policies for decision tables. As the name suggests, there can be multiple matching rows for a given input combination, i.e., multiple ‘hits’. All these rows are used to compute the output of the decision. While most decision tables use a single hit policy, there are situations in which a multi-hit policy allows us to define the decision logic in a more concise way. The DMN standard provides the following multi-hit policies. Output order (O): In theOutput orderhit policy, the output of the decision is an ordered list consisting of the output values of all matching rows. The order is defined by the order of the output values in the output value domain. This is analogous to the single hit policyPrioritythat uses the ordering of values in the output domain to derive the output value. Rule order (R): In theRule orderhit policy, the order of the output values is determined by the order of the matching rules. Collect (C): TheCollect hit policy returns an unordered list of output values; it can be extended with an aggregation operator that is applied on the output values. The hit policy C+ returns the sum of the values as decision output, C<the minimum value, C>the maximum value, and C# the number of values, i.e., the number of matching rows. An example of a decision table with the C+ multi-hit policy is given in Figure 5.19. The table decides on the number of annual leave days for employees. The output of the decision depends on the age of the employee and the years of service. The table reads as follows.

- Row 1: Any employee gets 26 days of annual leave.
- Row 2: If an employee is older than 30 years of age, regardless of the number of years in service, he or she receives another 2 days of annual leave.
- Row 3: Once an employee turns older than 60 years, 3 additional days of annual leave are granted.
- Rows 4–6: Depending on the years of service, up to 4 additional days of annual leave are granted.
Using a multi-hit policy, the decision table defines in a concise way the number of annual leave days. Depending on the input combination, up to all six rows can match. This holds, for example, for Anna, a 62 year old employee who has been in service for 31 years. In this case, the C+ hit policy returns the sum of output values of all rows, i.e., 40. The employee Berta, aged 24 who served for 3 years, would receive just 26 days of annual leave. Chris is 44 years old, and he served for 12 years for the company; he would receive 30 days of annual leave. To illustrate the other multi-hit policies, the cases of Anna, Berta, and Chris are revisited. Anna (62, 31) matches rows 1–6, resulting in Output order O: (2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 26), Rule order R: (26, 2, 3, 2, 3, 4), Collect summation C+: 40, Collect minimum C<: 2, Collect maximum C>: 26, and Collect count C#: 6. Berta (24, 3) matches only row 1, so that O: (26), R: (26), C+: 26, C<: 26, C>: 26, and C#: 1. Chris (44, 12) matches rows 1, 2, and 4. As a result, O: (2, 2, 26), R: (26, 2, 2), C+: 30, C<: 2, C>: 26, and C#: 3. While multi-hit policies provide a powerful mechanism to define decision logic in a concise manner, understandability might be hampered. Therefore it should be a conscious modelling decision if decision logic is defined using a single hit policy or a multiple hit policy. In many cases, theUniquesingle hit policy provides a high degree of understandability, while defining the logic of a decision precisely. It is worth mentioning that multi-hit policies do not add expressive power to decision tables. In fact, any decision table can be transformed to a decision table that can be evaluated using theUniquesingle hit policy. For details, the reader is referred to the bibliographical notes at the end of this chapter.

## 6 Process Choreographies
The previous chapter discussed how execution constraints between activities of a given business process can be captured in process orchestrations. However, dependencies do not exist only between activities of the same process orchestration, but also between activities of di!erent process orchestrations. This is the case if they participate in a business-to-business collaboration. To realize these collaborations, process orchestrations interact with each other, typically by sending and receiving messages. Choreographies have a central role in ensuring interoperability between process orchestrations, each of which is performed by a participant in a business-to-business collaboration. Several industry initiatives are in place for establishing standardized choreographies in particular domains. Examples include RosettaNet for the supply chain domain, SWIFTNet for financial services, and Health Level Seven (HL7) for healthcare services. They all define rules for the collaboration that companies need to comply with in order to collaborate with each other. By introducing collaboration rules, costs for the individual companies are reduced: interaction behaviour does not need to be agreed upon by every business partner, but, rather, industry-wide standards serve as reference for the intended collaboration. New companies can join the market more easily, since they know the rules of that domain. These collaboration rules are specified by process choreographies. While domain-specific process choreography standards are important in their particular fields, they lack the flexibility to define new types of business-to-business collaborations that are important for supporting cooperation between compa- nies in today’s dynamic market environments. Therefore, new approaches for the definition and implementation of process choreographies are required.
